So the fix wants the leader gate C33 applied, plus that ruling; a floor is not the missing input, and this entry no longer waits on one. **The `Empty` arm has the same shape and reaches the wire by a different door.** `read_redirect_response` resolves the leader first and returns *serve locally* before it ever consults `holds_room` — that gate is on the follower path alone — so a node **promoted** for a room whose state it never received is the leader, binds the channel, and answers `main` as empty for a room that has content on the node it took over from. What makes that the honest local answer rather than a fresh hole is that the same node serves that channel's live stream empty too, so the change list is still the diff of two states this reader is being handed; C51 declined to re-break the empty answer to paper over it, because a `holds_room` gate on the diff would refuse exactly the subscribed-but-never-written room C51 exists to serve. The routing is the missing piece, in both directions. Distinct from C51's taxonomy, which is about what a node reports for the state it has; this is about which node is asked. → *Server / Branches, Replication*.

**C104 — a snapshot fork whose base is gone silently becomes a live-log fork over `main` (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C51 (#393); split out of C53 on cubic's review, since it shares that entry's signature and neither its cause nor its ruling.** `owns_base` decides which kind of branch a name is by whether `branch_bases` holds an entry for it, and nothing ties that entry's life to the registry record beside it: `install_room` drops an orphan *base* whose branch is unregistered, and never the reverse. So a snapshot fork whose base is lost — a file gone where the branch record was not, or a `write_branch_base` that failed at a seam that treats it best-effort — reloads as a **live-log fork**, and `stream_doc` folds `main`'s log in `(floor, fork_point]` under the branch's name. `Some`, wrong bytes, no signal, and `publish` freezes them over the target's base and into a permanent version, which is C53's signature exactly and reached without any compaction. **Two conditions bound it**, and both matter to whoever rules on it: the room's floor must be 0, since above it the branch takes C51's `Unreadable` answer instead — so the defect lives precisely where C88's guard does not reach; and the lost base must not have *been* `main`'s own prefix at the fork point, which the ordinary `fork_branch_from_version` off a `main`-captured version reproduces harmlessly. What is genuinely wrong is an uncompacted room whose lost base was a `published` one, or a version captured off a restored branch — there the fork point lives in the *branch's* sequence space and `main`'s log at those numbers is other content entirely. The ruling is what `owns_base` should be: a registry flag the fork records at creation (so a missing base is `Unreadable`, C51's answer, rather than a different kind of branch), or the load path refusing a snapshot fork whose base did not come back. The first is the honest one and costs a persisted field; the second decides the branch's fate at load with no way to distinguish a lost base from one never written. Sits beside **C53**, which is the truncation route to the same wrong bytes, and **C88**, whose guard is what makes this one floor-conditional. → *Server / Branches*.

**C106 — a zone-named channel on a room whose acting schema declares *no* zones is served state the op seam withholds (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found by the cold review pass during C30 (#392), which closed the `None`-schema half of the same disagreement.** `zone_narrowing` declines for four reasons, and only two of them mean "nothing to narrow". `zones == None` (unfiltered) and "the set covers the declared range" (a whole-zone reader) are genuine. `schema == None` is the leak C30 closed by refusing the state read. **`declared == 0` is the same shape, still open**: a channel that named a zone against a schema that has since become one declaring none resolves to `Some({})` (`acting_zone_scope`'s fail-closed arm), the predicate `zone_scope_unprojectable` is false because a schema *is* present, `zone_narrowing` declines on `declared == 0`, and the state goes out whole — while `zone_admits(&Some({}), Some(k))` is `set.contains(&k)` = **false**, so every zone-stamped op is dropped on the live stream. Two seams, opposite answers, from one scope, which is precisely what C30 exists to prevent. The justifying sentence in `acting_zone_scope`'s doc was the tell and is now corrected: "every op is unzoned" is a property of the **log**, not of the acting schema, and holds only within one registered chain — the append-only rule guarantees it there, while a clone re-point (C28) swaps the acting *app* under a live channel and carries no such guarantee. **No confidentiality leak is demonstrated today**: `clone_room` refuses a `dst` already materialized, and a governed-source clone replaces the destination's content wholesale, so at the moment of a rebind there is no surviving zone-stamped content to serve. That is why this is filed rather than folded into C30 — the predicate is narrower than the invariant it enforces, but widening it refuses reads on a legitimately zoneless room and that is an availability ruling, not a bug fix. The obvious collapse does **not** work and the filing should not send anyone at it: at `declared == 0` with an empty set, `zone_narrowing`'s `whole` is *vacuously* true (`0 == 0`, and `(0..0).all(..)` holds), so "declines for a reason other than covering the declared range" does not catch this case — the four reasons are not disjoint. The distinguishing input is the **selector**, named versus whole-room, not the resolved set; that is also the cleaner rule, since a named selector against a zoneless schema is only ever reachable by drift (`zone_scope` refuses it outright at Subscribe). **And the reason to file rather than fix is the availability ruling, not the absence of content**: zone-stamped ops can reappear in the room *after* the rebind, since the channel's connection keeps writing under its own declared app and the server relays `op.zone` unchecked (C102) — so "no surviving zone-stamped content" is true only at the instant of the rebind. What holds is that widening the predicate refuses reads on a legitimately zoneless room, which is a ruling about availability rather than a bug fix. A second, simpler reachability path needs no governed source at all: a zone-named channel on app A, a second enforcing subscriber on a zoneless app B, an ungoverned clone dropping the binding, and the sweep's `pick_app` binding whichever id sorts first (C108) — if that is B, this state is reached with no zoneless source anywhere, and it is pinnable by a near-clone of `a_zone_named_channel_whose_room_loses_its_binding_is_refused_a_state_read`. → *Server / Zones*.

**C98 — the reveal back-fill never replays a revealed node's text, so a live reader materialises an empty run (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C16 (#391), identified by inspection.** `Hub::reveal_backfill` selects the log ops whose target sits in `Document::movable_subtree_containers(node)`, and that set collects each descendant XmlElement's *attrs* and *children* ids only — a Text run's own id is never in it, while `TextInsert`/`TextDelete` target the run. So a node revealed on move-in has its runs **created** and never filled: the `XmlInsertChild { tag: None }` that installs a run targets the children list and is back-filled, the text authored into it while the subtree was private is not, and the live reader holds an empty run where a fresh or snapshot joiner holds the content — the same permanent divergence C16 closes, one op kind over, and the same one the back-fill exists to prevent. The attrs half wants the same look: a run is the case inspection found, but the rule the set encodes is "containers reachable by the move", and every op kind that targets something inside a revealed subtree has to be in it or the reader diverges. Not reachable through today's authoring surface — no cursor addresses an already-materialised deep XML node, so a run inside a moved node can only be written by a raw op or by an earlier `insert_text` that the same transaction's move then relocates — which is why C16's fixtures are moves throughout, and why this is filed rather than folded into it. Wants the subtree set to name every container a relocated node carries, with a test that a revealed node's text arrives filled. Sits beside **C85**, which owns the branch axis of the same seam. → *Server / Fan-out*.

**C102 — an op's partition is its author's reading of the schema, and the server relays it unchecked (crates/server + crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found by the review pass during C30 (#392), which closed the reader's half of the same seam; pre-existing.** `op.zone` is stamped at the writing replica by `Document::zone_of_op`, against the schema *that client* holds, and the server never re-derives or validates it: the `Ops` ingest arm gates cross-zone *crossings* (`batch_zone_crossings`) and runtime-kind violations and touches the partition nowhere else; the only server-side write to `op.zone` is C16's reveal re-stamp on the fan-out. So the partition every reader is narrowed by is the author's word about a room the author does not govern. **Sharpest exactly where C30 leaves the fleet: mid-lift.** A room governed at v2, which declares `zb` over `/notes`, still holds v1 clients — v1 declares no zones, `zone_of_op` returns `None` for every edit, and `zone_admits` carries the root partition to every scope unconditionally — so a v1 client's write into `/notes` reaches a channel narrowed to `za` alone. The append-only rule (C30) makes ids *stable*, not *present*, so it does not reach this; the handshake range check admits the v1 client because the edge is invertible; and a rolling upgrade is the intended way to lift a `zones` block, which makes the window ordinary rather than exotic. The same shape reaches a **relay** writer (no schema, so every op it authors is unzoned) and any client whose declared version predates a zone's declaration. Not a C30 regression — a whole-room channel was served everything before it either — but C30 is what makes the reader's half exact and leaves this as the remaining door. **The fix is a ruling on which reading wins.** Re-deriving each op's partition at ingest against the acting schema (the server already builds `element_paths` on that path and `index::zone_of` already resolves it) makes the room's schema authoritative and costs a resolve per op; refusing a batch whose stamps disagree keeps the author's reading and turns the divergence into a recoverable `OpsRejected`, at the cost of stranding an in-range client mid-upgrade. Either way the per-zone lamport the envelope also carries is derived from the same field, so a re-derivation has to say what happens to a clock minted in the partition it is moved out of. → *Server / Zones*.

**C101 — a room whose governing version the registry does not hold is served to a whole-room channel unfiltered (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C30 (#392), which closed the sibling shape it shares a cause with.** A room's governing binding is `(app, version)` and outlives the process: `Registry::new_with_store` seeds `room_apps` from the hub's persisted bindings, while the `SchemaRegistry` is in-memory and is repopulated by the app's CI re-registering over the admin plane. Between a restart and that re-registration, `parsed_schema` resolves the binding to `None` — the version is simply not there — and `zone_scope` reads a whole-room selector against no schema as the one implicit root partition, so it returns `None`: no filtering. Every zone the room declares is served to every channel, the ones the deployment denies included, on precisely the seam §Zones promises an unauthorized partition is absent from. C30 closed the *stored-but-unparseable* door (registration now parses what it stores) and made a **named** selector fail closed to the root partition when it cannot be resolved; a whole-room selector cannot take that treatment on the same signal, because "no schema" is what a genuine relay room and an unbound room both look like, and narrowing those to the root partition would be a no-op there but is indistinguishable here. The fix wants the seam to tell "bound, unresolvable" from "unbound" — `Registry::deliver` already has that distinction in `room_binding` (`Some(Some(app))` vs `Some(None)`) and discards it before `session::step`, which takes only `Option<&Schema>` — and then to fail closed on the first: a bound room whose schema will not resolve serves the root partition alone until it does. That is a ruling about availability under a cold registry as much as about isolation, which is why it is filed rather than folded into C30. Shares its cause with **C62** (a room the hub holds that nothing ever bound — an import, a clone of an ungoverned room), which needs the binding to become room state that travels; this one needs no new state, only the distinction the resolver already computes. → *Server / Zones*.

**C85 — a branch fan-out synthesizes its reveal shells and their back-fill from `main`'s document (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C60 (#386), identified by inspection while closing the index axis it sits beside.** C60 moved the redaction *index* of a `(room, branch)` fan-out onto the branch's own tree. Two co-inputs to the same loop did not move: `Hub::reveal_ops(room, reads)` and `Hub::reveal_backfill(room, node, …)` both project `self.rooms[room].doc` — the live `main` replica — and both **synthesize ops** out of it. Reveal-on-move-in exists so a node dragged into a subtree a recipient can now read arrives with a shell it can materialize, plus the content authored while the node was private; derived from `main` on a branch stream, the shells describe a tree the recipient is not being served. Three readings, none of them benign: a node the branch holds and `main` has dropped gets **no** shell, so the recipient receives a readable `XmlMove` onto a node it cannot materialize — the exact strand the rule exists to prevent; a node `main` holds and the branch does not yields a shell for a node the branch's stream never places; and the back-fill replays *`main`'s* log records for the node's subtree, so a branch reader is handed content from a history its stream does not contain, which is a cross-stream leak rather than a materialization gap. The catch-up `Catchup::Ops` arm calls the same pair with the same argument. Unreached by C60's suite because reveal only fires for `XmlMove`, and a branch stream carrying a tree-move is the shape the residue needs. The fix is the same seam C60 built — resolve both against `Hub::stream_doc(room, branch)` — but the back-fill additionally needs the *branch's* records rather than `main`'s log, so it is a derivation change and not a re-pointing. `element_types` reads `main` on the same loop too and is deliberately **not** part of this: it narrows a type-scoped migration rewrite, not a read verdict. → *Server / Branches*.

**C86 — the doc-ACL write gate on a branch-bound channel resolves against `main`'s tree (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C60 (#386) by a review pass, identified by inspection; the write-tier mirror of the read seam C60 closed.** `session.rs`'s `Ops` arm builds `let index = hub.element_paths(&room)` and feeds it to `doc_acl_tier(…, Action::Write)`, while the channel's ops are routed to `ingest_branch` — so the tuple deciding whether the write lands is resolved through the live `main` tree and the write lands on the branch's. `scope_path` drops an `AclScope::Element` tuple whose id the index cannot resolve, so an element-scoped **write-deny** naming a container that stands only on the branch — one the branch base holds and `main` has dropped, or one born on the divergent tail — resolves to nothing, goes inert, and the write is admitted by whatever the deployment and schema tiers say. The allow direction has the mirror: an element-scoped write *grant* over a branch-only container is likewise inert, so an authorized editor is refused. Pre-existing (C60 changed only the read seams) and mechanically the same fix — `hub.stream_element_paths(&room, &branch)` off the channel's bound branch, with C60's `None` ⇒ refuse rule, since a stream this node cannot fold cannot be write-gated either. What it needs on top of a re-pointing is a decision the read seam did not: the write gate runs per inbound batch on the leader's hot path, so it inherits C60's held tree rather than folding one, and a *write* is what grows that tree — the ordering of "gate the batch, then append it" against "the tree the gate reads must not already contain the batch" has to be stated. The **cross-zone move token** seam has the same wrong tree (`session.rs`'s `CrossZoneToken` arm resolves the moved element through `hub.element_paths(&room)`), and belongs here: it fails *closed* — a branch-only element resolves nowhere and no token is issued — so it is an availability bug rather than a leak, but it is one re-pointing away from correct once this unit decides the ordering. → *Server / Authz*.

**C87 — `rename_branch` moves a branch's pointer and leaves its tail and owned base under the retired name (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C60 (#386) by a review pass, identified by inspection; pre-existing on `main`.** `Hub::rename_branch` calls `mutate_branches(registry.rename(from, to))` and nothing else. `branch_logs` and `branch_bases` are keyed by branch *name*, and no rename site touches either — the only mutators are `install_room`, `delete_branch`, `repoint_published` and `ingest_branch`. So a renamed branch loses its divergent tail and, for a snapshot fork, its owned base: the new name catches up as a bare live-log fork over `main`'s log, silently dropping every op the branch diverged by, and a published branch renamed becomes a live-log fork of `main` rather than a frozen snapshot. The orphans stay on disk and in memory under the old name, where `delete_branch` can no longer reach them, so a later fork reusing the retired name inherits them — the very shape `delete_branch`'s own cleanup comment says must not happen ("so a later fork reusing the name never inherits stale ops"). Wants the rename to carry both maps and both store files with the pointer, under the same persist-before-commit discipline the other mutations take. C60 made it a redaction input as well as a serve one — the tree a branch read is redacted against is folded from exactly those two maps — which is why it is filed rather than left implicit. → *Server / Branches*.

**C88 — compaction destroys a live-log fork's shared base, and the branch then serves a document with no history (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C60 (#386) by a review pass, reproduced.** A live-log fork owns nothing: `catch_up_branch` reads its base straight out of `main`'s **retained** log, in `(last_seen, fork_point.min(head)]`, guarded by `base_end > r.base_seq`. `Hub::compact` folds that log into the replica and lifts `base_seq` to the head, so the guard fails and the branch's catch-up returns its divergent tail alone — a stream whose base has silently evaporated. A fresh subscriber to it materializes an empty-then-tail document, diverging from every peer that joined before the compaction, and then edits from it. Reachable with no operator action wherever `set_compaction_threshold` is configured, since `ingest` compacts inline once the log passes it; `main` itself is unharmed (its replica carries what the log folded into it) and a snapshot fork is unharmed (it owns its base), so this is the live-log fork's alone. **Two escalations a review pass measured on top of that.** The reach is not compaction's alone: `install_room_state` installs a whole replica with an *empty* log at a raised floor, so **every node that took a state transfer** — the ordinary below-floor follower catch-up — clips every live-log fork it holds, with no compaction threshold configured anywhere. The consequence was not confined to a bad read either, and that half is now closed: C60 (#386) made a clipped shared base yield no tree, so every seam reading `Hub::stream_doc` refuses rather than resolving against one that reaches less than the branch holds — the redaction indexes, and with them `materialize_branch`, so a **publish** of a clipped fork freezes nothing and (since C51) a diff of one answers `UnreadableBranch` rather than reporting the branch absent, both pinned in `branch_state_taxonomy`. What stands is the read: `catch_up_branch` still answers `Catchup::Ops` with the divergent tail alone, so a fresh subscriber is still caught up to a base-less document and edits from it, and the branch is now unservable at every *other* seam besides — a refusal is not a repair. The fix is to give every live-log fork an owned base **wherever the retained log is dropped below a fork point** — compaction and the snapshot install both: materialize each registered fork whose fork point sits above the new floor into `branch_bases` before the log goes, under the same persist-before-commit rule `fork_branch_from_version` takes, at which point the branch is a snapshot fork and neither seam concerns it again. Note the cost is real and is the ruling to make — one whole-replica copy per live-log fork per room, paid at the compaction that would otherwise reclaim space. → *Server / Branches*.

**C89 — a fork taken off a branch shares `main`'s log, not the source branch's stream (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C60 (#386) by a review pass, reproduced.** `Hub::fork_branch(room, new, from, at)` clamps `at` to the **source's head** and then records `new` as an ordinary live-log fork, whose base `catch_up_branch` reads out of `main`'s log in `(floor, fork_point.min(main.head())]`. A branch head counts its divergent tail, so forking off a branch that has diverged produces a `new` whose fork point names a sequence in the *source's* space and whose base is drawn from `main`'s — two different histories under one number. Two readings, both wrong. The branch never inherits the source's divergence: `b1` writes ten ops, `b2` forks off `b1`, and `b2`'s stream carries none of them, which is not what forking a branch means anywhere. And the fork point lands **above** `main`'s head, so `min` clamps it to a moving target and `main`'s later writes keep flowing into `b2`'s base until `main` catches up — a branch that silently absorbs the trunk's history for a while and then stops. C60 made both observable rather than introducing either: the redaction tree is folded from the same derivation, so its shared-base window has to record `main`'s head to stay correct (it does), and a window that grows forces a full refold per `main` write on that branch instead of the tail-forward fold every other branch gets. The fix is that a fork's base is its **source's stream**, not `main`'s: either give the new branch an owned base materialized from the source at the fork point (making it a snapshot fork, which is what `fork_branch_from_version` already does for a version), or teach the catch-up to walk a fork chain. The first is the smaller change and composes with C88, which wants live-log forks to own bases anyway. → *Server / Branches*.

**C90 — a branch stream's migration translation resolves its element types from `main` (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C60 (#386) by a review pass, identified by inspection; the migration-tier sibling of the seams C60 closed.** Both the live fan-out (`registry.rs`) and the `Catchup::Ops` arm (`session.rs`) build `hub.element_types(room, schema)` — a walk of the **`main`** document — and hand it to `translate_ops_scoped` / `translate_delta_scoped`, which use it to narrow a **type-scoped** migration step to the ops whose owning element is of the step's declared type. A container standing only on the branch (in its owned base, or born on its divergent tail) has no entry there, so the step silently skips its ops and a cross-version recipient is served the field **untranslated** — the failure mode a type-scoped step exists to prevent, arriving with no error. The snapshot half of the same catch-up compounds it: `catch_up_snapshot` → `translate_snapshot` takes no type projection at all, so the two joins for one branch translate differently and the op-join ≡ snapshot-join convergence a type-scoped step is documented to preserve does not hold there. Out of C60's scope because it narrows a rewrite rather than a read verdict — C60 moved every input that decides *who* sees an op and left the one that decides *what shape* they see it in — but it is the same wrong tree. Fix is the same re-pointing (`element_types` over `Hub::stream_doc(room, branch)`) plus a decision the read seam did not need: what the *snapshot* seam narrows by, since it must agree with the op seam over the same branch state. → *Server / Migration*.

**C91 — a follower's default subscribe takes the `main` fast path whatever branch the room's active HEAD names (crates/server) — READY, needs a ruling. Found during C60 (#386) by a review pass, identified by inspection; pre-existing on `main`.** `read_redirect_response` decides whether a follower may serve a read from its own replica, and its stream test is `branch.is_empty() || branch == MAIN_BRANCH`. The empty case is there because an empty branch *used* to mean `main` — since restore-as-branch it means **the room's active HEAD**, which a restore switches to the restored branch. A leader mirrors only `main` to its followers (the comment says so, and branch replication is a later unit), so after a restore a plain subscribe answered by a follower is being served the one stream that node is guaranteed *not* to hold. The obvious re-pointing does not close it: `hub.active_branch(room)` is durable per node and is **not replicated**, so the follower does not know the restore happened and resolves the empty name to `main` anyway. That leaves two real options and the ruling is which. **Replicate the active-HEAD pointer** — make it room state that travels, as C29 made the creator travel — after which the resolution is correct on every node and a named-branch redirect covers the rest; this is the same shape C62 wants for the room's app binding. **Or refuse the fast path for an unnamed branch** (`branch == MAIN_BRANCH` alone), which is one character and strictly safe, and costs follower read-serving for every client that does not name `main` explicitly — i.e. the common case, which is most of the point of the feature. C60 resolves the branch name earlier in the subscribe than it used to but deliberately left this gate reading the raw name, because moving it there would silently pick the second option's failure mode on nodes that do not know the pointer. → *Server / Branches*.

**C92 — a fan-out that must withhold a whole stream tells its existing subscribers nothing (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C60 (#386) by a review pass, identified by inspection.** C60 made `fan_out_ops_redacted` return without sending when the `(room, branch)` stream has no tree to redact against, and the subscribe seam refuse a *joiner* on the same branch with `"branch state is unreadable"`. A channel already bound gets neither: it keeps its subscription, receives nothing, and has no way to tell that from a quiet room — so it believes it is live while its replica silently stops advancing, which is the shape C5 and C59 both established is a delivery bug rather than a self-healing one. The trigger is ordinary rather than exotic: `install_room_state` lands an empty log at a raised floor, so **every node that takes a whole-replica state transfer** clips every live-log fork it holds, with no compaction threshold configured anywhere (C88). Wants the withholding seam to close the affected channels — a `Message::Error` plus a forced unsubscribe, or an `EngineEvent` the runtime turns into one — so the client reconnects into the refusal the subscribe path already gives it, rather than being left holding a stream nobody is feeding. Note the decision it needs: the fan-out is per batch and the condition is per stream, so the signal has to be raised once and not on every withheld write. → *Server / Fan-out*.

**C93 — a one-member partition mints a spendable transaction key for no atomicity (crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C2 (#390); pre-existing, made routine by it.** `tag_atomic` accepts `(1..=MAX_TX_MEMBERS)`, so a commit of one edit — and, since C2, *each singleton partition of a straddling commit* — ships tagged at `count == 1`. A count-1 group is behaviourally identical to an untagged op at every receiver: it buckets, `take_complete_tx` completes it on the arrival that created it, and it meets the same readiness gate. What it does buy is cost: a permanent entry in the unbounded, persisted `resolved_tx` at the author *and* at every receiver that commits it (C20's retention question, whose slope C2 already steepened), and a group id of `TxId::derive([seq])` — a pure function of **one** sequence, so after `free_seq` recycles a sequence (a write the room refused, or one a filter withheld from catch-up) a later singleton re-derives the *identical* id rather than colliding at the 64-bit birthday bound `TxId::derive`'s contract assumes. **Not a divergence** — that is why C2 left it alone rather than folding it in: a count-1 bucket completes on the arrival that opens it, so a repeated id is resolved-key stray handling, exactly the path C21 (#372) built, and every order lands the same set. The candidate fix is a floor of `(2..=MAX_TX_MEMBERS)`, so a partition of one streams untagged — removing the key, the persisted growth and the one-sequence id surface with no observable difference at any receiver. It is a ruling about the **cap**, not about the zone constraint, and while no receiver could tell the difference, it rewrites an envelope four shipped specs assert. Measured by running the floor change: `zone_tx_split::an_op_whose_governing_region_is_ambiguous_joins_the_root_partitions_group` (the straddling mark's lone `RangedCreate` is the one singleton partition in C2's own suite), `transaction::two_atomic_transactions_get_distinct_ids`, and `convergence::atomic_groups_do_not_change_what_ops_merge_to` and `::rewritten_group_counts_converge_under_every_order` (both assert *every* op rides a group). Two of the four are in the convergence suite rather than a zone one, which is the argument for its own decision: the floor is a property of the cap, and it is asserted where the cap is exercised. → *Transactions / Scope Constraints*.

**C97 — a schema rebind inside an open transaction re-indexes the zone ids the commit is cut on (crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C2 (#390); pre-existing, raised in consequence by it.** `zone::zone_id_of` returns the *index* into `Schema::zones()`, and `Document::set_schema` is callable while `self.atomic.is_some()` — it is exposed on every binding (`crdtsync_doc_set_schema`, wasm, Python, Go), so an application can reach it. Rebind mid-commit and `op.zone` on the ops emitted before and after the rebind denote **different zones** while carrying the same `u32`. Before C2 that mis-stamped a lamport clock; now `tag_atomic` buckets on the raw `u32`, so it also silently groups two different zones' ops under one transaction id — the exact straddle C2 exists to prevent, reached without any cross-zone edit. `set_schema`'s rustdoc already states the precondition ("Bind at a settle point, not inside an open atomic transaction") and `take_repairs` already *enforces* its own version of it by returning empty while a transaction is open, so the seam and the convention are both there; only the guard is missing. Wants `set_schema` to refuse (or defer) while a transaction is open, on whatever signal the binding family already uses for a refusal, plus a test that a mid-commit rebind cannot merge two partitions into one group. → *Core / Zones*.

**C84 — a follow-on op of an annotation or an ACL tuple is stamped where the region is *now*, so a cross-zone move splits the family (crates/core) — READY, needs a ruling. Found by the review pass during C74 (#387), which introduced the split shape.** C74 made an op's partition the region it governs, resolved through the live tree at emit. An `AclScope::Element` is exactly a region that can move: a cross-zone tree move is a supported, token-authorized flow, so a grant on an element in `za` is stamped in `za` and a revoke authored after the move is stamped in `zb`. The `za`-scoped subscriber received the grant and receives neither the move nor the revoke, so it holds a live `Allow` tuple a freshly-projected snapshot of the same zone set does not carry; the `zb`-scoped one receives the revoke with no grant, the readiness gate holds it (it wants `acl` to name the id), the zone projection's buffer filter retains it, and it rides that replica's `encode_state` indefinitely. The same shape reaches a `RangedSetPayload`/`RangedDelete` whose anchored sequence has crossed. Distinct from **C82** (there the region resolves to no path at all; here it resolves perfectly, to a different zone than the family's earlier op) and from **C83** (a keyed zone-root slot). Not new *divergence* — `project_zones` keys an ACL tuple by its scope's **current** zone, so the projection has always answered "where is it now" while op delivery answers "where was it then", and pre-C74 an element-scoped grant on a zoned element rode the root to every subscriber while the projection dropped it. What C74 changes is that the family now *splits*, where before both ops rode the root together. **The ruling is what a follow-on op inherits.** Stamping it from the partition its family was *created* in makes the family self-consistent and is the only rule under which an op-served and a snapshot-served replica of one zone set agree — but the create records no partition today, so it is new persisted state (the bar C21's resolved-key set and C41's shell section are held to), and `project_zones` would have to key the tuple and the annotation by that recorded partition rather than by the scope's current one, which is a second ruling on what a projection may serve a subscriber whose zone the element has left. Bounded reachability: a cross-zone move needs a server-sealed capability token, so this is an explicitly privileged operation's residue, not an ordinary write's. → *Core / Zones*.

**C83 — a keyed op naming a zone-root slot rides the parent's partition unless it is a create (crates/core) — READY, needs a ruling. Found by the review pass during C74 (#387); pre-dates it.** Zones Unit 3 (#278) moved a container-*create* to the created child's partition, so a zone owns its own root container's creation and an unauthorized subscriber never sees it appear. The rule is keyed on `create_child_key`, which names the five create kinds alone, so every other keyed op naming the same slot still resolves through the *parent* map's path. Measured on `zones: {za: "/board", zb: "/notes"}`: `MapDelete { key: "notes" }` and `MapSet { key: "notes", value: Int(42) }` both carry `zone = None` and fan out to a za-only subscriber — the zone root's *deletion*, and its replacement by a scalar, announced to a subscriber that never saw it created. §Zones says an unauthorized client observes "not even a clock jump or a signal that the zone exists", so this is that signal on the seam Unit 3 half-closed. Same shape one slot down: a `RegisterSet`/`CounterInc`/`CounterDec` at a key whose extended path falls in a nested zone. **The op half is a one-liner** — a `slot_key(kind)` covering the keyed non-creates beside `create_child_key`. **The state half is the ruling**: `project_zones` detaches an unauthorized zone-root slot only where the child `is_container()` (the detach loop reads `child.is_container() && purge.contains(&child.id())`), so a *scalar* sitting at a zone-root key survives every zone projection and no purge id names it — the two seams would part company in the other direction the moment the op seam tightens. Closing it means the projection cutting a slot by the zone its extended path resolves to, whatever occupies it, which is a rule about slots rather than about elements and is why this is filed rather than folded into C74. → *Core / Zones*.

**C82 — an op whose governing region resolves to no path keeps the root partition while the projection drops the state form (crates/core) — READY, needs a ruling. Filed by C74 (#387), which closed the resolvable case; numbered above C80, the top of the range the in-flight C60 (#386) files into, since C7 (#385) has already taken C75 and #386 will have to renumber its own claim on it — so this block owes no renumber on either side.** C74 made an op's partition the region it governs — a `Ranged*` op's anchors', an `Acl*` op's scope's — so the op seam and the snapshot projection now withhold the same state from the same subscribers wherever that region resolves. Where it does **not** they still part company: an anchor sequence the live walk does not reach (a deleted or displaced container's), an element scope whose target has left the tree, and a `Path` scope that is not a `core::path` (raw bytes, unvalidated on the way in) each name no partition, and so does a mark whose two endpoints straddle zones. C52 (#383) ruled the projection **drops** such state, since "names no zone" would read as the root partition every zone-scoped subscriber holds. The op seam cannot say the same thing: `Option<u32>` has no value for "no partition" — every value it takes names a per-zone clock, so a third envelope state needs a clock of its own, and riding a zone's would advance a partition the op does not belong to. So the op keeps the root and reaches every zone-scoped subscriber, carrying a tuple's subject and effect, or a mark's name and payload, out of a region no verdict can place. **It also splits an op family across two partitions, which is new**: a mark's create is stamped in its zone, and a later payload change or delete authored *after* the anchored sequence's container is deleted resolves to no partition and rides the root — so a subscriber scoped away from the zone holds the later op without ever having held the create, the readiness gate holds it (it wants `ranged`/`acl` to name the id), the zone projection's buffer filter retains it (`op.zone.is_none_or`), and it rides that replica's `encode_state` indefinitely. **And the fallback partition is a mint floor, so the split loses writes, not just audience.** The root clock on a replica that has authored nothing in it sits far below the zone the family's create was stamped from, and a `RangedSetPayload` is LWW on the stamp — so a payload change written after the anchored sequence's container is gone is minted *under* the payload it means to replace and is discarded, convergently, on every replica. The same inversion reaches any LWW write inside a mark's composite payload container. Pre-C74 the whole family rode the root together and the write landed, so this is the one place the unit trades a lost update for the leak it closes, and the reason C82 is a regression to close rather than a corner to note. Pinned by `a_follow_on_that_falls_back_to_the_root_partition_mints_below_its_familys_clock`. Raising the root mint floor over the zone clocks is not the fix — that publishes a zone's clock level into the partition every subscriber holds, which is the activity channel §Zones closes. The element-scoped `AclGrant`/`AclRevoke` pair has the same shape. Narrower than what C74 closed — this state is already inert for enforcement (the evaluator's resolver drops such a tuple on the same lookup) and unreachable for a mark until its region is deleted or displaced — but with **C84** it is one of the two remaining disagreements between the two seams (this one where the region resolves to nothing, C84 where it resolves somewhere else). **The ruling is what the envelope should say**: a third partition state with its own clock and its own `zone_admits` arm (admitted only to an unfiltered subscription, matching "the caller declines to project"); or the server re-deriving such an op's audience from the room's tree the way `op_read_gate` already does, accepting that it costs the seam the envelope was designed to avoid; or keeping the root and narrowing the *snapshot* to match, which reverses C52. Whichever wins, ARCHITECTURE §Zones' per-zone wording has to state it. → *Core / Zones*.

**C75 — a call on a closed SDK handle is reported as whatever the C ABI's empty-buffer signal means at that entry point (sdks/python + sdks/go) — READY, no dependencies. Found by the review pass during C7 (#385).** Both wrappers null their handle on close (Go `Client.Close` sets `c.h = nil`, Python drops `_handle`) and no method guards against being called afterwards; the C ABI's null-handle branch returns the same empty `CrdtBuf` a genuine refusal does. Inert while every buf-returning method treated empty as "nothing to report" — a call after close silently answered `b""`/`nil`. C7 is what makes it visible: `subscribe` now reads an empty frame as the core declining to assign a channel, so a subscribe on a closed client raises `ChannelsExhausted` / returns `ErrChannelsExhausted` — a true failure reported under a false cause, and the remediation it names (open a session under a fresh client id) is not the fix for it. Every future entry point that gives the empty buffer a specific meaning inherits the same conflation, so the question is the general one: a use-after-close is a caller bug the SDKs currently answer by silently degrading, and the fix wants one convention — a guard raising `ValueError`/returning an `ErrClosed` at every entry point, or a documented refusal to guard — not a special case at `subscribe`. Scoped to the two `ctypes`/cgo wrappers; wasm/JS hold no closable handle of this shape. → *SDK / Lifecycle*.

### Correctness follow-ons from the C1 review (found 2026-07-26, out of C1's scope)

**C70 — every reader of the live room-app map drops a dormant room's schema while the frame authz path keeps it (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found by the review pass during C49 (#382).** Two answers exist to "which schema governs this room" and they disagree. `Registry::deliver`'s `room_binding` reads the live `room_apps` map and falls back to `hub.governing_app(room)`, so a room a dormant sweep or a restart dropped from the live map is still governed by its own persisted app. Every *other* reader takes `room_apps` alone: `governing_schema` (the op-outbound redaction, the awareness fan-out, `authorize_blob_fetch`) and, separately, `resolve_schema_policy` and `resolve_throttle`, which read the map directly and never go through it. So for a room the sweep dropped, those seams reach `acl::authorized` with no schema — and resolve no awareness TTL or throttle — while an authorizing *frame* against the same room reaches it with one. Sharpest at the blob gate, which scans every room the hub holds and so meets dormant ones by construction: a reader whose only read authority is a schema grant is refused a blob it should reach. The fan-out and the two policy resolvers need presence, which is what keeps a room in the live map, so they are narrower. Fail-closed in each direction, so a second gap of C49's class rather than a hole. The fix is the hub fallback at **every** reader — the divergence exists because each grew its own reader — plus a decision on whether they should simply be one resolver. **Partly closed by C30 (#392): `governing_schema` has the fallback, and with it every reader that goes through it** — the plain and redacted op fan-outs (so `peer_may_read` *and*, since C30, each channel's zone scope), the awareness set and removal fan-outs, and `authorize_blob_fetch`. That reader was forced there rather than chosen: once a channel's zone scope is re-resolved per use, a fan-out reading no schema for a dormant-but-bound room *widens* the channel to every partition, which is a leak rather than C70's filed fail-closed. **Five direct readers of `room_apps` remain**, and the C30 pass found three the original filing did not name: `resolve_schema_policy` and `resolve_throttle` (a dormant room resolves no awareness TTL and no throttle), `fire_schedule_triggers` and `drain_auto_versions` (its `autoVersion` triggers never fire), and `translation_source` (its fan-out serves ops verbatim instead of translating them). **The live window is narrower than the filing implies**, which is worth knowing before picking this up: `reconcile_room_apps` drops a room only when it holds neither presence nor a subscriber, an enforcing subscribe re-binds within its own delivery (`step` runs on the hub fallback, `bind` repopulates the map after it), and a restart *seeds* `room_apps` from the persisted bindings. So the remaining readers are stale exactly while a room is held only by **relay** connections — where translation is moot (a relay recipient is never translated) and the residue is the TTL, the throttle, and the auto-version triggers. A bound room whose version the registry cannot resolve is the different defect C101 owns. What is still open is those five readers plus the ruling on collapsing them into one resolver.

**C62 — a room the hub holds but nothing ever bound reads as having no zones (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found by the review pass during C28 (#376), reproduced by inspection.** Every zone verdict is `Resource::Zone { room, zone }` and every *enumeration* of a room's zones comes from its governing schema, resolved through the room's `{app_id, version}` binding. The binding is made on **subscribe**, so a room that has never had a subscriber has none: an `import_room` template (the canonical clone source — imported precisely so it can be copied, never joined), a clone of an already-ungoverned room, and a promoted replica (replication carries the creator, C29 #374, but not the binding, and a follower's `persist_meta` writes `governing: None`). At that point `reads_every_zone` — and `zone_scope`, and `zone_narrowing` — read "no schema" as "declares no zones", which is right for a **relay** room (one implicit root partition, and it must stay clonable) and wrong for a zoned room whose binding was never made. C28 closed the two shapes where the *caller* supplied the schema and the one where a *replica's* view did; this is the one where nobody's view has it. Reachable on a single node, where the leadership check is a no-op: in the canonical zone deployment (room read allowed, one `Resource::Zone` denied) the denied actor clones the imported template and subscribes to the copy. **C54 (#396) closed the replication half:** the binding rides `Replicate` and `ReplicateSnapshot` beside the creator and the op-version high-water, so a promoted replica — and a follower no client ever subscribed to — resolves the room's zones from the room's own binding. It took a different shape than this filing proposed, deliberately: the adopt is a `Registry` seam routed through `bind_room_app` rather than an `install_room_state` argument, because that is where the incumbent-app rule lives and duplicating it inside the hub would give one field two composition rules. **What is left is the case with no binding to travel**, which is this filing's second half and the ruling in it: the `import_room` template (the canonical clone source, imported precisely so it can be copied, never joined) and a clone of an already-ungoverned room. Bind at import from an operator-supplied app, or refuse to serve a zone-bearing read of a held-but-unbound room. Subsumes the C30 sibling shape at this seam (a stored binding whose schema will not parse resolves to no schema too). → *Server / Zones*.

**C22 — a global mint floor leaks cross-zone activity through a zone's lamport numbering (crates/core) — READY, needs a human ruling. Raised by C17 (#366).** A stamp is a document-global id, so the mint has to clear the replica's whole id space; every partition-scoped rule C17 tried was defeated by an op's envelope naming its own partition while its ids land in its target's, or by a snapshot declaring any clock it likes. The consequence is that a zone's lamports count on from the author's whole stamp position, so the gaps between one author's stamps inside a zone reveal how much it minted outside — the activity-inference channel ARCHITECTURE (§Isolation) says per-zone clocks close. Folding is untouched, so the streams stay causally independent and separately replicable, and the leak is *numbering*, not content or structure. **The ruling is which guarantee wins**, since correctness holds either way: keep the leak; or give the decoder a trustworthy way to attribute a decoded stamp to a partition (the only label that arrives with one today is the attacker-supplied envelope, and the honest one — `zone_of_op` over the target's path — needs a schema a decode does not have); or scope the mint per partition and accept that a peer can aim it at a victim's live ids. Whichever wins, ARCHITECTURE's zone-isolation wording needs to match it. → *Core / Zones*.

**C44 — two inserts at one stamp carrying *different* tags derive one child, whose tag is the first arrival's (crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C24 (#371), reproduced by inspection; pre-dates C24.** `xml_child_id` mixes the children list, the stamp and the *kind* into the derivation, but not the tag — so `XmlInsertChild { tag: Some(b"div") }` and `XmlInsertChild { tag: Some(b"span") }` at one stamp into one list derive the **same** `XmlElement` id. `registered_handle` materialises it with `entry(id).or_insert_with(|| XmlElement::new(id, tag))` and no op ever rewrites a tag, so the element renders `div` delivered one way and `span` the other, and the two replicas encode different bytes. C24 makes the two claims a join rather than a contest — correctly, since they name one node — and converges the *position*; the identity content it does not reach. **Mixing the tag into the derivation is not the fix**: C24's rank leans on `born_at` being pure in the key, and a stamp derives exactly two children (the tagged and the tagless) precisely because the derivation carries no payload — a tag in it would make "is this node the child this key derives" unanswerable without guessing every tag. So the tag has to resolve by an intrinsic order over the two ops (the smaller tag bytes, say), which means a seam that can restate a materialised element's tag and a decision about what that does to a live handle an app already holds. The tag rides the snapshot, so whichever rule is chosen is recoverable on reload without new persisted state. → *Core / XML*.

**C45 — a birth that loses its key while holding a move placement loses its created-under edge on reload (crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C24 (#371).** C24 re-seeds the created-under relation from three sources — a node's birth placement, its parent link when it holds no placement at all, and one hop up `parents` for everything else — which covers a losing birth that was left placeless. A node born at `(Lb, sb)`, *then moved* to `(L2, s2)`, and only afterwards evicted from `(Lb, sb)` by a smaller-id twin falls through all three: `birth_placement` finds nothing, `placements` is non-empty so the placeless source skips it, and its `parents` entry is the move destination (a children list) so the one-hop source skips it too. `evict_placement` returns early without re-pointing anything, because the node still holds a placement. The live replica keeps `base[node] = owner(Lb)`; a replica reloaded from its snapshot has no entry at all. While the move edge governs, `parent_of` agrees and nothing shows; withdraw that edge — a later eviction at `(L2, s2)`, or a projection that filters it — and the live replica falls back to the birth owner while the reloaded one reads the node as a root, `refold_moves` skipping it entirely and leaving its suppression flags at whatever the last fold wrote. It is also a hole in the tree the move log's cycle check walks. Nothing in the snapshot still names the birth owner, so the fix is to persist the edge (a base-edge section in the state codec, or the birth list beside each placement record) — new persisted state, which wants the decision made explicitly rather than inherited, the same bar C21's resolved-key set is held to. → *Core / XML*.

**C43 — two moves at one stamp into *different* children lists resolve by arrival order (crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C24, reproduced; pre-dates C24.** Filed during C24 (#371) and renumbered from C30, which C15 (#368) had already taken. C24 ranks the claims that contend for one `(list, stamp)` key. Two `XmlMove`s carrying one stamp into *two* lists contend for nothing — each takes a free key, both `Claim::Fresh` — and the divergence moves one layer down instead: `TreeMoves::apply` dedups on the stamp alone, so the second `moves.apply` returns `false` and that node records **no edge at all**. Measured: `[into_b, twin]` folds to `frag(a(y()),b(x()))` and `[twin, into_b]` to `frag(a(x(y())),b())` — one op set, two trees, and which node is left edgeless is exactly the arrival order. Admissible for the reason C24 gives: dedup is on `OpId`, and the id-space record only bounds an honest mint. The fix is the same shape as C24's, applied to the log rather than the placement — key the entry `(stamp, child)` so two nodes never collide, or keep the stamp key and tiebreak the dedup on child id so the surviving edge is a function of the ops. Note the log is persisted, so whichever is chosen has to hold on replay as well as on the wire. **C24's eviction path is a second route into the same dedup**, found by cold review on #371: `evict_placement` withdraws the loser's edge by an exact `(stamp, child, parent)` match, so `[m1, m2, birth]` (m1 into L, m2 into L2, the birth outranking at L's key) leaves the log empty and folds the node back to its birth list, while `[m2, m1, birth]` leaves `(stamp, node, owner_of_L2)` standing and renders it in L2 — one op set, two trees. Matching loosely is not the fix (withdrawing an edge this claim did not make is a divergence of its own); keying the log so two nodes never share a slot closes both. → *Core / XML*.

**C42 — a create arriving after a reveal and a move renders the node under two parents (crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C24, reproduced; pre-dates C24 and reproduces on `main`.** Filed during C24 (#371) and renumbered from C29, which C15 (#368) had already taken. `insert_xml_child` overwrites `parents[child]` with the birth list unconditionally and re-folds only when its claim joined or evicted (before #371, only when it evicted), so a node the move log already governs — materialised by an `XmlReveal` and relocated by the move that reveal exists to carry — has the fold's verdict discarded when its *real* create lands afterwards, and a second live sequence node installed beside the one it already renders as. Measured: `[birth, reveal, move]` folds to `frag(a(x(),y()),b(c()))` and `[reveal, move, birth]` to `frag(a(x(),y()),b(c()),c())`, the node under two parents at once — the duplication the Kleppmann fold exists to forbid, and a divergence between two replicas that saw the same three ops. The reveal/create overlap is what makes it reachable: a partial reader is served a shell for a node born where it cannot read, and the real create reaches it later if the redaction changes or a peer replays it un-projected. Fix is to re-fold whenever the born node was already materialised, so the birth placement joins the record and the fold picks the live one, rather than assuming a fresh birth governs nothing. Distinct from C24's rank: the two ops do not contend for one key, they name one node from two seams. → *Core / XML*.

**C41 — a reveal shell does not survive a snapshot, so a restarted replica refuses the move that places it (crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C24, reproduced; pre-dates C24.** Filed during C24 (#371) and renumbered from C28, which C15 (#368) had already taken. `Document::revealed_pending` is the set of movable nodes materialised by an `XmlReveal` shell and still awaiting their first placement — the server injects those shells so a partial reader can hold a node born in a subtree it cannot read. Both the readiness gate and `apply_move` accept a move of such a node *because* it is in that set. Nothing encodes it: `encode_state` writes no section for it and `read_state` rebuilds it empty, so a replica that snapshots between the shell and the move that places it comes back with the shell materialised and unplaceable, and every later move of it buffers forever while a peer that stayed up applies it. Measured directly — reveal, snapshot, reload, move — one replica renders the node under its new parent and the other does not. Independent of C24 but sharpened by it, and **C24's shell case is conditional on this unit**: C24 makes a node left with no placement movable again, and a decode re-derives that from the parent link — which recovers a *born* loser but not a **shell**, since a shell has no such link to key on and C24 correctly removes the one the fold gave it. So `a_reveal_shell_that_loses_the_placement_stays_movable` holds live and across both arrival orders, but not across a snapshot: reveal a shell, let a smaller-id mover evict it, reload, and a later move of the shell buffers where the live replica applies it. That is this unit's hole reached by a new route, not a new hole — the plain shape (reveal, snapshot, reload, move) diverges on `main` too — but it is the reason C24 cannot be called complete for shells until this lands. The fix is a section in the state codec for the shells (and the projections' filters for it, since a shell's whole point is that its origin was denied), which is new persisted state and wants the decision made explicitly rather than inherited — the same bar C21's resolved-key set is held to. → *Core / XML*.

**C40 — two ops carrying one stamp into one plain list resolve by arrival order (crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C24, reproduced.** Filed during C24 (#371) and renumbered from C27, which C15 (#368) had already taken. C24 closed the *children-list* half: a placement key is owned by a birth over a move, then by the smaller element id, whatever the arrival order. One layer down the same collision is still first-wins. `List::insert_at` is idempotent on the id, so two `ListInsert` ops under one `ClientId` carrying the identical `Stamp` into one list — both admissible for the reason C24 gives, dedup being on `OpId` and the id-space record bounding only an honest mint — leave whichever landed first holding the slot, with its value *and* its Fugue anchor. Delivered the other way the other value renders, and the two replicas' `encode_state` bytes differ (measured: the encoded node carries `2` on one and `99` on the other). The C24 tiebreak does not carry over: a scalar node has no element id to order by, so the rule has to be a total order over the *values* (their encoded bytes, with the kind tag as discriminator), and it belongs in `List` rather than the document, which means it reaches `Text` runs on the same seam. Two shapes to settle first: whether the winner's *anchor* travels with its value (C24 says yes — the tombstone that a delete leaves must not remember the loser's position), and what a scalar contending with a composite node resolves to, since a plain `ListInsert` and an `XmlInsertChild` can name one stamp in one children list and only the latter passes through `placement_index`. #371 sharpened that half rather than closing it: a claim owns the key, not the id, so `Fresh` finds `insert_at` inert against a scalar already at the id while an eviction or a join re-seats over it — `[ListInsert, tagged, tagless]` replaces the scalar and `[ListInsert, tagless, tagged]` keeps it, one op set and two trees. The rule has to say what a composite claim does to an id a scalar holds, not only who owns the key. → *Core / List*.

**C47 — a minority `count` rewrite folds one op set to two states, because unanimity is judged over the members that have *arrived* (crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C21 (#372); pre-existing on `main` before it. Filed under a different id during that unit and renumbered into C21's reserved range, its first id having been claimed by a sibling unit in flight.** C3 bounds a rewrite with "a bucket whose members disagree names no group and is never complete", and C21 leaned on that. It bounds rather than closes, because a unanimous **subset** can reach its own declared count before the dissenting member lands. **Reproduction:** take an honest three-member group and rewrite **two** members to declare 2, leaving the third at 3 — every member legal on its own terms, `is_admissible` passes. Delivered `x,y,z` or `y,x,z` the pair completes at 2, commits, spends the key, and `z` lands as a stray of a resolved group: all three present. Delivered in any of the other four orders the dissenter is in the bucket from the start, `tx_declared_count` returns `None` forever, and **nothing lands at all**. Two distinct states over six orders, measured. **C21 (#372) widened it**, and this entry should not read as though it left it alone: spending the key on the minority's commit makes the stray land, which is *visible* on pools where the un-recorded replica's commit cancelled itself out and the stray stayed held. Measured over 389 byte-identical forged pools x 12 orders, the split rate goes 58 -> 85 — 28 pools read two ways here that read one way before, 1 the other way. Forged envelopes only, and eviction still collapses all 85 to a single reading. It is the cheapest of the four rewrite shapes for an attacker, since it rewrites a minority rather than every member. **The fix is one rule, and it is a reversal:** a bucket whose members disagree can never honestly complete, so it **spends its key** where a commit spends it — every order then lands all three and spends the key, and the no-duplicate convergence fuzz passes. What it costs is C3's decision that a disagreement means *hold*: `a_rewritten_first_member_count_does_not_commit_the_group_at_the_wrong_size`, `a_bucket_whose_members_disagree_on_the_size_never_completes` and `a_rewritten_count_holds_the_same_set_whatever_order_it_arrives_in` all pin the opposite and would invert, and ARCHITECTURE §Opt-In: Atomic's unanimity sentence changes with them. Distinct from **C46**, which needs per-op-id evidence; this one needs only the rule to change. ARCHITECTURE already carries the caveat that unanimity bounds rather than closes, so the docs do not overclaim in the meantime. → *Transactions*.

**C48 — a recipient served a destranded group never spends its key, so it holds a stray the author and a whole-delivery recipient merge (crates/core + crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C21 (#372); the one shape C21's record *opens* rather than closes. Filed under a different id during that unit and renumbered into C21's reserved range, its first id having been claimed by a sibling unit in flight.** The record is per-replica **evidence**, and ARCHITECTURE §Opt-In: Atomic says every filtering seam destrands the survivors of a group it cuts — so the recipient that most needs the key is the one with no evidence to earn it. **Reproduction:** author `A` mints atomic `T = {m1, m2}` and spends `(A,T)` at the mint; `R_whole` receives both tagged, commits, spends `(A,T)`; `R_cut` receives both untagged (what `Session::zone_filter` and `Document::project_zones` deliver a zone-scoped subscriber) and spends nothing. A forged stray — an unrelated op of `A` re-tagged `(T, 2)`, the same envelope rewrite C21 exists for — then merges at `A` and `R_whole` and is **held** at `R_cut`. On `main` all three held it alike, so this pair agreed before the record and disagrees after; it is the one cost C21 adds rather than removes. It needs the forged op, so it is attacker-triggered rather than ordinary traffic, and **eviction (C20) closes it** — which is another reason C20 is a correctness policy rather than cleanup. **The other way is for the destranding seams to spend the keys they cut**, which they know: `project_zones` computes `split_groups` before calling `destrand_split`, and `Session::zone_filter` has the same set per batch. Two things to answer first. A snapshot projection currently *clears* the record whole so a subscriber cannot count the groups a withheld partition resolved — re-adding the straddling keys reveals that those groups existed, which is a narrower leak but a real one, and the naive `extend(split)` also re-adds groups that fell **wholly** inside the withheld partition, which is exactly what the clear exists to hide. And the live wire seam has no channel to tell a receiver "this key is spent" without a protocol frame. → *Transactions*.

**C46 — a second envelope of one op id that the buffer holds nothing to contradict (crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C21 (#372); pre-existing on `main` before it. Filed under a different id during that unit and renumbered into C21's reserved range, its first id having been claimed by a sibling unit in flight.** C21's record spends a key on evidence the buffer is *holding*, so it closes a second envelope that **disagrees with the copy the buffer holds** — in group id or in declared size — and misses every shape where there is nothing tagged to disagree with. Two ways in, and the second needs no forgery at all. **(a) The envelopes name different groups.** Deliver `X` tagged `(T2, 2)` beside a genuine member of `T2`: that bucket completes and applies `X`. Deliver `X` tagged `(T1, 2)` afterwards and it is a plain duplicate of an id already applied — the buffer holds nothing under `T1` to contradict, so nothing is spent, and `T1`'s own genuine member waits on an id that will never join it. Deliver the two copies the other way round and the conflict is caught in the buffer, both keys are spent, and every member lands. **Why C21 stopped here.** Closing it means spending a key on evidence from an id merely *applied*, and that trades this law for another: a resend is ordinary traffic on any transport that retries, so a delivery that spent a key would make state a function of how often an op arrived — and a fabricated group id on such a duplicate grows the persisted record without bound. Both were measured on C21's first pass. **What it would take** is per-op-id evidence rather than per-key: recording which group a member was *committed under* (bounded by the dedup set, one entry per committed tagged op) so a later envelope naming a different group is answerable without trusting the arriving tag. That is a second persisted structure, and it wants the same explicit decision C21's record got. **(b) The other envelope carries no tag at all** — which is exactly what ARCHITECTURE says every filtering seam produces, so this half is reachable on honest traffic rather than by rewrite: an honest three-member group plus a destranded bare copy of one member folds to **2 observable states** over its 24 orders (measured, and identical on `main`), because a bare copy that lands first is applied with no group recorded and the remaining members hold on a count they can never reach. `buffered_tx` returns `None` for a held-but-untagged op and an applied one is not buffered at all, so neither reaches the conflict rule. Until then the members converge on eviction (**C20**) and only the spent-key record differs; the (a) half is pinned as `a_copy_absorbed_by_another_groups_bucket_strands_its_own_group_until_eviction`. → *Transactions*.

**C20 — nothing calls the partial-transaction eviction seam (crates/server + crates/core) — READY, no dependencies. Found during C3 (#363), by grepping for callers.** `Document::evict_partial_transactions` is the give-up path for a group no arrival will complete, and C3 deliberately shipped the seam without a policy: the core reads no clock, so *how long to wait* is the caller's. Nothing calls it — not `crates/server`'s room replica, not `ClientSession`, not an SDK — so the retention C3 bounds for the shapes a local test can name is still open for the ones it cannot: send one member each of N groups, distinct `TxId`s, in-range unanimous counts, and every op is buffered for the life of the replica and re-persisted into every snapshot. There is no size cap on `Document::buffer` either. C2 (#390) multiplies the *other* unbounded set's growth rate: a commit now spends one `resolved_tx` key per zone partition it spans rather than one per commit, so the persisted record grows by up to `zones+1` entries per straddling commit — same retention question, a steeper slope. Wants a room-maintenance tick on the server side and a session-level equivalent — **C21 (#372) removed the prerequisite this entry was written around.** Eviction now spends the bucket key it gives up on, so a tick landing between two members of an *honest* group untags it and the member that follows is a stray of a spent key rather than the first member of a fresh group: measured over 400 pools x 11 tick placements, replicas ticking out of phase converge on what a reader sees, where `main` before it diverged on the visible value. So a bare period no longer needs an age input or an evict-only-what-was-already-waiting contract to be safe for what a reader sees — what remains is the period itself, whether the client evicts on resume, and three narrower residues: a replica that never evicts still does not converge with one that does; a single tick placement can leave a member unread until the next tick, so it is the repeating policy that carries the guarantee rather than one tick; and the buffer of members untagged but not yet ready still differs by tick placement, so snapshot *bytes* are not preserved even where the reading is. Note the sharper reason it is not optional: **a replica that never evicts does not converge with one that does** (ARCHITECTURE §Opt-In: Atomic), so this is a correctness policy, not a cleanup. → *Transactions / Sessions*.

**C64 — a reported durable head can raise a watermark past a withheld write without releasing its ack (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C57 (#380), measured.** `Registry::release_pending_acks` is driven from `record_replica_ack` and (since C57) from the reap — but not from the third input that moves a quorum: `catch_up_follower_reporting` sets a follower's watermark through `set_watermark`, which can move it *up* to `reported_head.min(hub.seq(room))` and so past a withheld write's sequence, then returns without a release pass. The client's `Accepted` waits on a subsequent genuine ack an idle room may never produce, and is dropped unanswered if the connection goes first. Wants the release pass driven from that seam too, with a test that a rejoining follower reporting a head at or past the withheld write's sequence lands the owed `Accepted` with no further write. → *Cluster*.

**C65 — the ack watermark map is bounded on the node axis and open on the room axis (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C57 (#380).** C57 keys `Replication::acked` on the roster, but `record_ack` still inserts `(room.to_vec(), follower)` for whatever room bytes a peer names — a room this node does not lead, one whose placement has since moved off that follower, or one that never existed. So a peer that is authenticated to the replication plane (cluster secret) but buggy or compromised grows the map on fabricated room ids, and ordinary placement churn leaves entries for rooms a follower no longer replicates; neither is struck until the *member* is reaped. The sibling insert path is already bounded — `set_watermark` runs under `rooms_led_for` — so the fix is to gate `record_replica_ack` on `replicas_for(room).contains(follower)` rather than plain membership. Not `is_effective_primary_for`, which flaps with liveness and would strand a withheld ack for a room whose leadership momentarily moved. → *Cluster*.

**C69 — a minority that reaps the rest of the cluster shrinks its own quorum and releases writes the true majority never sees (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C57 (#380).** `Registry::quorum` computes the majority over whatever the current roster places, with no floor at the replica-set size the writes were accepted against; `Membership::is_stranded` catches only the reap-to-one case. So a node on the minority side of a partition that reaps the members it cannot reach — a bounded dead-time away, and the whole point of reaping — recomputes a room's set from 5 to 2 and its majority from 3 to 2, then releases `Accepted` for writes the majority side never received, while that side leads the same rooms. The stranded guard names exactly this hazard for a ring of one and stops there; a ring of two has the same majority-of-one-side property against a five-member cluster. Wants a quorum floor that a reap cannot lower below the configured replication factor (or an explicit operator opt-out for a deliberate shrink), and a test that a reap-driven minority refuses rather than releases. **This owns the reap seam's answer too:** C57 built a reap-driven release pass and reversed it on exactly this ground — the pass can realistically fire only once the ring is below the replication factor, so a release there is the hazard rather than a fix — leaving a withheld ack held until the floor is settled. Sits beside C36, which covers the availability half of the same partition. → *Cluster*.

**C39 — adoption evidence is first-hand and never relayed, so a compromised member that speaks selectively splits the ring permanently (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C25 (#369), measured, and pinned there as a passing test (`a_compromised_member_that_speaks_selectively_splits_the_ring`).** **Read this first: C25's central guarantee — that placement is identical on every node — does not hold against a compromised *adopted* member.** That is the guarantee the unit was written to establish, and it holds against every attacker except this one. The disagreement is **permanent, not eventually-consistent**: it survives unbounded further anti-entropy, because the mechanism is a claim that was never sent rather than one that was sent late. Measured with 5 honest nodes and one compromised member over 1000 rooms: **721/1000 rooms placed differently, 149 rooms in which two honest nodes each believe themselves primary, 111 rooms one node holds a replica of that the other denies it holds.** Reachability is what makes it *permanent*, not what makes it exist — against a candidate every honest node can reach, the identical trick still splits the ring, and merely heals one gossip period later (measured: 164/1000 rooms). C25 was merged with this open on the ground that this residual is strictly dominated (which holds for *this* one, though not for C25 as a whole — see C36): the same attacker on a cluster without C25 obtains a superset of this capability — it grinds an id, places itself into any room's replica set unilaterally, supersedes the leader with a forged epoch, and makes majority-ack release a client `Accepted` for a write no majority holds — so no threat model comes out worse, and holding the fix would have preserved the easier attack rather than a safe state. C25 does nonetheless add a failure *dimension* that did not exist before, because the member set converges and the verifier set does not. C25 made the ring a function of `configured` + `verifiers` and disseminated the *evidence* rather than the verdict, precisely so that placement would be identical on every node. Liveness converges because every node re-gossips what it heard. `verifiers` does not: `known_liveness` emits one bit per member and that bit is `verified_by_self`, so a node passes on nobody else's word. That was deliberate — with no signature on the wire, "A and B verified X" is free for any node to write, and relaying unsigned claims would hand a compromised member a full mint rather than a split. The cost is that the one component placement depends on has no exchange protocol between nodes: a claim reaches exactly the nodes its maker chooses, and no honest node can carry it further or contradict it. So a compromised **adopted** member is a swing vote for any candidate that exactly one other trust unit has reached — it sets the `verified` flag toward some peers and clears it toward others, both frames well-formed and correctly attributed, and the two groups place rooms differently. Measured with 5 honest nodes and one attacker over 1000 rooms: **721/1000 rooms placed differently, 149 rooms where two honest nodes each believe themselves primary, 111 rooms one holds that the other denies it** — and the split survives 30 further full anti-entropy sweeps with the attacker silent, because there is no path by which the withheld claim could arrive. Those rooms are permanent `gate_replica_frame` → `Reject` → link-drop loops. For a joiner every honest node *can* reach the disagreement instead heals in one sweep (measured: 164/1000 rooms for one gossip period), so reachability is what makes it permanent, not what makes it exist. C35's unrefutable eviction chains into it: reaping strips a member from `configured`, so it returns as a pending joiner needing two units, handing the attacker a swing vote at every re-admission. **The fix is evidence a receiver can attribute without trusting the relay:** a claim signed by the verifier's peer key — which every node already holds under mTLS and every receiver can already check against that member's certificate — carried as `(verifier, target, signature)` instead of one self-scoped bool. Relaying then converges by union keyed on the *original* verifier, and a forging relayer gains nothing. The alternative is to stop deriving the ring from locally-held evidence and make adoption an agreed decision on the existing epoch/leadership machinery. → *Cluster*.

**C34 — a member that owns a host owns every ring position under it, because placement keys on node ids while a certificate names a host (crates/server) — READY, no dependencies. Found by cold review during C25 (#369) and pinned there as a passing test (`a_certified_member_still_mints_ids_on_its_own_host`).** C25 made rooms place on *adopted* members and made adoption the cluster's decision, which closes minting on anyone else's host, minting an id nobody can reach, and any single member manufacturing the evidence. What it cannot close is the mint on a member's **own** host: HRW scores a node id, C13 binds a link to the *host* a certificate names, and a host answers at as many ports as it likes — so a compromised member grinds `own-host:<port>` until HRW places it on the room it wants, answers there, and every honest node that dials it verifies it **truthfully**. No evidence rule can help, because no one is lying.
